"excerpt_sha256": "35dc088d1fb2984530999ae14d37a71ff205bfb58b5e2ac5b98dc9bc2d30d43d", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0026-E018", "source_id": "SRC-c4f3d695fa7d2630", "url": "https://www.cecc.gov/publications/commission-analysis/ongoing-challenges-faced-by-persons-with-disabilities-in-the-people", "title": "Ongoing Challenges Faced by Persons with Disabilities in the People’s Republic of China", "publisher": "Congressional-Executive Commission on China", "raw_sha256": "a926b1fa5fd098d9ae0625a26cc88be94c5a60399ec28d30872cab88c89d9720", "fact_summary": "A 2021 study found rural hukou was associated with a 13-40% increase in unmet healthcare needs among persons with disabilities, who were over 79% rural residents. In April 2023, a court sentenced the husband in the Feng County chained-woman case to nine years and five traffickers to 8-13 years, after public criticism and reported earlier official failures to protect the victim.", "question_ids": ["3B.7", "3B.9", "6B.10", "6B.2", "6B.3", "6B.8", "8B.8"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The healthcare study is pre-2023; the court event occurred in April 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The court action is an institutional act under the PRC state; no personal Xi involvement is shown.", "limitations": ["The case demonstrates a remedy after severe harm but does not establish systemic prevention or nationwide enforcement.", "The study’s healthcare estimate predates the target period."], "start_unit": 11, "end_unit": 14, "locator": "extracted HTML block 11 through extracted HTML block 14", "exact_excerpt": "Rural residents are confronted with an additional layer of difficulty, as they have a significantly higher rate of poverty and have access to fewer healthcare resources and less robust infrastructures, as compared with urban residents.\n[66]\nThese gaps are exacerbated by the mandatory household registration (\nhukou\n) system, which designates a place of registration as either urban or rural and ties access to resources (such as medical care, health insurance, and social welfare) to a person’s place of registration.\n[67]\nAccording to a 2021 study, “rural\nhukou\nwas associated with an important 13–40% increase in unmet health-care needs for people with disabilities in China.”\n[68]\nThe implication of this is significant because over 79 percent of persons with disabilities are rural residents.\n[69]\nBesides the urban-rural divide, the\nhukou\nsystem excludes access to resources by the “floating population,” a term used by authorities to primarily refer to rural residents who have moved to urban areas for work but lack a “local hukou at their de facto place of residence . . . .”\n[70]\nWhile central authorities in 2014 abolished the urban-rural distinction for household registration purposes,\n[71]\nthe change did not achieve equal access to healthcare because only a small number of internal migrants were eligible to move their\nhukou\nto a city, and facilities in rural areas continued to lag behind.\n[72]\nAs of 2021, the government did not have a national policy specifically focused on providing protection and services to persons with disabilities who are part of the floating population.\n[73]\nIn addition, the scope of the healthcare needs of persons with disabilities within the floating population is poorly understood by Chinese authorities, according to a 2021 editorial in the CPPCC Daily authored by a district-level people’s political consultative conference from Chongqing municipality.\n\n[74]\nThe floating population is estimated to have reached 376 million in 2020,\n[75]\nbut the number of people with disabilities among the migrant population is not known.\n[76]\nThe CPPCC Daily editorial recommended that the central government conduct a census to determine the number of people with disabilities in the floating population; assess the scope of the population’s health, rehabilitation, and social integration needs; and develop national policy and implementation mechanisms specific to persons with disabilities.\n[77]\nV. Mishandling of Emergency Response during the COVID-19 Pandemic\nThe death of a teenager with cerebral palsy in January 2020, six days after his father and sole caregiver was quarantined on suspicion of COVID infection, exposed inadequacies and lack of sensitivity in China’s emergency response during the COVID-19 outbreak.\n[78]\nDuring this time, the teenager was only fed twice and multiple emergency calls made by his father and volunteers did not yield medical assistance.\n[79]\nA paper pointed out that the PRC Law on Prevention and Treatment of Infectious Diseases “did not incorporate considerations for the needs of people with disabilities in situations of risk and humanitarian emergencies,” falling short of the requirements under Article 11 of the CRPD.\n[80]\nIn addition to the shortcomings in the legal framework, the implementation of the government’s COVID measures “[paralyzed] transportation, businesses, logistical circulation and many more services,” causing disruption to the provision of “supplies and services including the assistive devices, timely medication [and] caretaking services” needed by persons with disabilities.\n[81]\nVI. Vulnerability of Women with Psychosocial Disorders to Trafficking and Domestic Violence\nWhile the Chinese legislature has enacted laws to prohibit offenses against persons with disabilities,\n[82]\nlegal provisions that specifically protect women with psychosocial disabilities from abuses such as domestic violence and human trafficking are still lacking. For example, the October 2022 amendment to the PRC Law on the Protection of Women’s Rights and Interests does not substantially strengthen protections for women with psychosocial disabilities.\n[83]\nMoreover, according to a civil society report entitled “Leaving No Sister with Disabilities Behind,” existing legal provisions create a conflict that makes it “difficult to file a case when the victim’s right is infringed by the guardian, including one’s spouse.”\n[84]\nThe report states that “[t]he right to life of women with intellectual or psychosocial disabilities is oftentimes threatened rather than protected when entering marriage,” and “[s]ometimes the public security and civil affairs departments both shirked responsibility and did not act properly . . . .”\n[85]\n\nIn one example, a family of three tortured and caused the death of a new bride who was reported as having an intellectual disability.\n[86]\nBefore her death, local police declined to intervene, treating the matter as “a domestic affair between a legitimate husband and wife . . . .”\n[87]\nAdditional datapoints illustrate the extent of the problem:\nAn analysis of 616 judicial case files involving trafficking in women from 2017 to 2020 shows that 20% of the 1,252 women trafficked in China had disabilities . . . . And more than 60% of the women with intellectual or psychosocial disabilities were abducted by strangers passing by in outdoor locations such as roadsides, stations, and fields. Another study of 1,038 first-instance judgments involving the crime of trafficking in women and children from January 2000 to July 2017 shows that 26.7% of the victims suffered from varying degrees of mental illness.\n[88]\nIn one widely publicized case, a woman reported to be mentally impaired was shown in a video chained in a shed in Jiangsu province.\n[89]\nThe woman, originally from Yunnan province, reportedly was sold several times before being sold to her current husband, surnamed Dong, with whom she had eight children.\n[90]\nPublic criticism drew attention to the local government’s apparent disregard for human trafficking,\n[91]\nthe violation of the national birth limit policy, and the woman’s abusive living conditions,\n[92]\nthe inconsistencies in the official investigative reports about her background,\n[93]\nand lax criminal penalties for “buyers” of trafficked persons.\n[94]\nIn April 2023, a court sentenced Dong to nine years in prison for “abuse” (\nnuedai\n) and “illegal imprisonment” (\nfeifa jujin\n) of the woman, noting the deterioration of her mental illness during multiple pregnancies.\n[95]\n\nThe court sentenced five of the traffickers to terms of imprisonment ranging from 8 to 13 years.\n[96]\nHuman Rights Watch used the case to illustrate the wider problem of bride trafficking, pointing out that China’s previous population control policy, which permitted couples to have only one child and caused a sex imbalance due to male child preference, along with the availability of sex-selective abortion, drove the internal and cross-border trafficking of women.\n[97]\nMany victims come from vulnerable communities, and it is difficult for international organizations to monitor the abuse due to heavy government censorship.\n[98]\nVII. Conclusion\nWhile the PRC has taken steps to assist persons with disabilities, the various safety nets devised by the government have been shown to be insufficient in terms of amount and access. This may be attributed to institutional and political factors, including the exclusion of civic groups and the intended beneficiaries from the design and implementation of these assistance programs due to the government’s continuing authoritarian control over the distribution of resources. Additionally, social control policies such as the household registration system and the lack of effective counter-human trafficking measures render certain subsets of the disabled population particularly vulnerable. The PRC government, therefore, should not only increase the amount and types of assistance, but it should also undergo meaningful policy and structural reforms to accommodate greater adherence to the international standards as laid out in the CRPD.\n[1]\nU.N.
